<div style="line-height: 1.6em; font-weight: bold;"><div>Influences</div></div>
<ul class="mw-collapsible-content" style="margin-top: 0; margin-bottom: 0; line-height: inherit; list-style: none; margin-left: 0;"><li style="line-height: inherit; margin: 0"><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;"><a href="/wiki/Edmund_Husserl" title="Edmund Husserl">Edmund Husserl</a>, <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Reinach" title="Adolf Reinach">Adolf Reinach</a>, <a href="/wiki/Max_Lehmann_(historian)" title="Max Lehmann (historian)">Max Lehmann</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Ferreira_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ferreira-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Martin_Heidegger" title="Martin Heidegger">Martin Heidegger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Ingarden" title="Roman Ingarden">Roman Ingarden</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Hering&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Jean Hering (page does not exist)">Jean Hering</a>, <a href="/wiki/Richard_H%C3%B6nigswald" title="Richard Hönigswald">Richard Hönigswald</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Ferreira_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ferreira-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Oswald_K%C3%BClpe" title="Oswald Külpe">Oswald Külpe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Max_Scheler" title="Max Scheler">Max Scheler</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Henry_Newman" title="John Henry Newman">John Henry Newman</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ignatius_of_Loyola" title="Ignatius of Loyola">Ignatius of Loyola</a>, <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Erich_Przywara" title="Erich Przywara">Erich Przywara</a></div></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr class="note"><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><div class="collapsible-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed" style="text-align: left;">
<div style="line-height: 1.6em; font-weight: bold;"><div>Influenced</div></div>
<ul class="mw-collapsible-content" style="margin-top: 0; margin-bottom: 0; line-height: inherit; list-style: none; margin-left: 0;"><li style="line-height: inherit; margin: 0"><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;"><a href="/wiki/Edmund_Husserl" title="Edmund Husserl">Edmund Husserl</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hedwig_Conrad-Martius" title="Hedwig Conrad-Martius">Hedwig Conrad-Martius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Martin_Heidegger" title="Martin Heidegger">Martin Heidegger</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Emerita_S._Quito&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Emerita S. Quito (page does not exist)">Emerita Quito</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Ingarden" title="Roman Ingarden">Roman Ingarden</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Gerda_Walther&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Gerda Walther (page does not exist)">Gerda Walther</a>, <a href="/wiki/Erich_Przywara" title="Erich Przywara">Erich Przywara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Karol_Wojtyla" class="mw-redirect" title="Karol Wojtyla">Karol Wojtyla</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Reinach" title="Adolf Reinach">Adolf Reinach</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alasdair_MacIntyre" title="Alasdair MacIntyre">Alasdair MacIntyre</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Anselm_Min&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Anselm Min (page does not exist)">Anselm Min</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dermot_Moran" title="Dermot Moran">Dermot Moran</a></div></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Edith Stein</b> (religious name <b>Saint Teresia Benedicta a Cruce</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">OCD</a></span>; also known as <b>Saint Teresa Benedicta of the Cross</b> or <b>Saint Edith Stein</b>; 12 October 1891 – 9 August 1942) was a <a href="/wiki/German_Jewish" class="mw-redirect" title="German Jewish">German Jewish</a> philosopher who converted to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholicism</a> and became a <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">Discalced Carmelite</a> <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nun</a>. She is canonized as a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> and <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>; she is also one of six <a href="/wiki/Symbols_of_Europe#Patron_saints" title="Symbols of Europe">patron saints of Europe</a>.
</p><p>She was born into an observant <a href="/wiki/Jews" title="Jews">Jewish</a> family, but had become an <a href="/wiki/Agnostic" class="mw-redirect" title="Agnostic">agnostic</a> by her teenage years. Moved by the tragedies of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, in 1915, she took lessons to become a nursing assistant and worked in an infectious diseases hospital. After completing her doctoral thesis at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Freiburg" title="University of Freiburg">University of Freiburg</a> in 1916, she obtained an assistantship there to <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Husserl" title="Edmund Husserl">Edmund Husserl</a>.
</p><p>From reading the life of the reformer of the <a href="/wiki/Carmelite_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Carmelite Order">Carmelite Order</a>, <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Edith Stein was drawn to the Christian faith. She was <a href="/wiki/Baptized" class="mw-redirect" title="Baptized">baptized</a> on 1 January 1922 into the Catholic Church. At that point, she wanted to become a Discalced Carmelite nun but was dissuaded by her spiritual mentor, the abbot of <a href="/wiki/Beuron_Archabbey" title="Beuron Archabbey">Beuron Archabbey</a>. She then taught at a Catholic <a href="/wiki/School_of_education" title="School of education">school of education</a> in <a href="/wiki/Speyer" title="Speyer">Speyer</a>. As a result of the requirement of an "<a href="/wiki/Aryan_certificate" title="Aryan certificate">Aryan certificate</a>" for <a href="/wiki/Civil_servant" class="mw-redirect" title="Civil servant">civil servants</a> promulgated by the Nazi government in April 1933 as part of its <a href="/wiki/Law_for_the_Restoration_of_the_Professional_Civil_Service" title="Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service">Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service</a>, she had to quit her teaching position.
</p><p>Edith Stein was admitted as a postulant to the Discalced Carmelite monastery in <a href="/wiki/Cologne" title="Cologne">Cologne</a> on 14 October, on the first <a href="/wiki/Vespers" title="Vespers">vespers</a> of the feast of Saint Teresa of Ávila, and received the <a href="/wiki/Religious_habit" title="Religious habit">religious habit</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Novice" title="Novice">novice</a> in April 1934, taking the <a href="/wiki/Religious_name" title="Religious name">religious name</a> <i>Teresia Benedicta a Cruce</i> (Teresia in remembrance of Teresa of Ávila, Benedicta in honour of <a href="/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia" title="Benedict of Nursia">Benedict of Nursia</a>). She made her temporary vows on 21 April 1935, and her perpetual vows on 21 April 1938.
</p><p>The same year, Teresa Benedicta a Cruce and her biological sister Rosa, by then also a convert and an extern (tertiary of the Order, who would handle the community's needs outside the monastery), were sent to the <a href="/wiki/Carmelite_Monastery,_Echt" title="Carmelite Monastery, Echt">Carmelite monastery</a> in <a href="/wiki/Echt,_Limburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Echt, Limburg">Echt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>, for their safety. In response to the pastoral letter from the Dutch bishops on 26 July 1942, in which they made the treatment of the Jews by the Nazis a central theme, all baptized Catholics of Jewish origin (according to police reports, 244 people) were arrested by the Gestapo on the following Sunday, 2 August 1942. They were sent to the <a href="/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp" title="Auschwitz concentration camp">Auschwitz concentration camp</a>, where they were murdered in a <a href="/wiki/Gas_chamber" title="Gas chamber">gas chamber</a> on 9 August 1942.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg/220px-Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="259" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg/330px-Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg/440px-Bad_Bergzabern-St_Martin-36-Edith_Stein-2019-gje.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2813" data-file-height="3309" /></a><figcaption>Icon in Bad Bergzabern. The scroll shows a quote from her works: "The innermost essence of love is self-offering. The entryway to all things is the Cross"</figcaption></figure>
<p>Edith Stein was born in <a href="/wiki/Breslau" class="mw-redirect" title="Breslau">Breslau</a> (now <a href="/wiki/Wroc%C5%82aw" title="Wrocław">Wrocław</a>, Poland), <a href="/wiki/Lower_Silesia" title="Lower Silesia">Lower Silesia</a>, into an observant <a href="/wiki/Jewish" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish">Jewish</a> family. She was the youngest of 11 children and was born on <a href="/wiki/Yom_Kippur" title="Yom Kippur">Yom Kippur</a>, the holiest day of the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_calendar" title="Hebrew calendar">Hebrew calendar</a>; these facts combined to make her a favorite of her mother.<sup id="cite_ref-VNS_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-VNS-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> She was a very gifted child who enjoyed learning, in a home where her mother encouraged critical thinking, and she greatly admired her mother's strong religious faith. By her teenage years, however, Stein had become an agnostic.
</p><p>Though her father died while she was young, her widowed mother was determined to give her children a thorough education and consequently sent Edith to study at the <a href="/wiki/Schlesische_Friedrich-Wilhelms-Universit%C3%A4t" class="mw-redirect" title="Schlesische Friedrich-Wilhelms-Universität">Schlesische Friedrich-Wilhelms-Universität</a> in Breslau. At age 19, Stein moved with her family to Breslau to a house bought by her mother, which she later described in her <i>Autobiography</i>. Today, Edith Stein House hosts a museum dedicated to the history of the Stein family.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Academic_career">Academic career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Academic career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In April 1913, Stein arrived at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_G%C3%B6ttingen" title="University of Göttingen">University of Göttingen</a> in order to study for the summer semester with <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Husserl" title="Edmund Husserl">Edmund Husserl</a>. By the end of the summer, she had decided to pursue her <a href="/wiki/Doctoral_degree" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctoral degree">doctoral degree</a> in philosophy under Husserl and chose <a href="/wiki/Empathy" title="Empathy">empathy</a> as her thesis topic. Her studies were interrupted in July 1914 because of the outbreak of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>. She then served as a volunteer wartime <a href="/wiki/Red_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Red Cross">Red Cross</a> nurse in an infectious diseases hospital at <a href="/wiki/Hranice_(P%C5%99erov_District)" title="Hranice (Přerov District)">Mährisch Weißkirchen</a> in 07.04 untill 1.9. 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> In 1916, Stein moved to the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Freiburg" title="University of Freiburg">University of Freiburg</a> in order to complete her dissertation on <a href="/wiki/Empathy" title="Empathy">Empathy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Shortly before receiving her degree from Freiburg she agreed to become Husserl's assistant there. In this role, she differed with Husserl on important issues and made independent contributions to phenomenology as a whole. <sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Her dissertation entitled <i>Das Einfühlungsproblem in seiner historischen Entwicklung und in phänomenologischer Betrachtung</i><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> (<i>The Empathy Problem as it Developed Historically and Considered Phenomenologically</i>)<sup id="cite_ref-SEP_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SEP-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> was awarded a doctorate in philosophy with the <i><a href="/wiki/Summa_cum_laude" class="mw-redirect" title="Summa cum laude">summa cum laude</a></i> honor.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Stein then became a member of the faculty at Freiburg, where she worked until 1918 as a <a href="/wiki/Teaching_assistant" title="Teaching assistant">teaching assistant</a> to Husserl, who had transferred to that institution.<sup id="cite_ref-SEP_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SEP-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The University of Göttingen rejected her <a href="/wiki/Habilitation_thesis" class="mw-redirect" title="Habilitation thesis">habilitation thesis</a> in 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-SEP_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SEP-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Although Stein passed her doctoral examination with distinction, her attempts to habilitate failed due to the fact that Stein was a woman.<sup id="cite_ref-SEP_12-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SEP-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Her rejected habilitation thesis, <i>Beiträge zur philosophischen Begründung der Psychologie und der Geisteswissenschaften</i><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> (<i>Contributions to the Philosophical Foundations of Psychology and the Human Sciences</i>), was published in the <i>Jahrbuch für Philosophie und phänomenologische Forschung</i> in 1922.<sup id="cite_ref-SEP_12-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SEP-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> She is categorized as a <a href="/wiki/Phenomenology_(philosophy)" title="Phenomenology (philosophy)">realistic phenomenologist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While Stein had earlier contacts with Catholicism, it was her reading of the autobiography of the mystic <a href="/wiki/Teresa_of_%C3%81vila" title="Teresa of Ávila">Teresa of Ávila</a> during summer holidays in <a href="/wiki/Bad_Bergzabern" title="Bad Bergzabern">Bad Bergzabern</a> in 1921 that prompted her conversion and eventually the desire to seek the life of a <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelites" title="Discalced Carmelites">Discalced Carmelite</a>. Baptized on 1 January 1922, and dissuaded by her spiritual advisers from immediately seeking entry to the enclosed and hidden life of a Carmelite nun, Stein obtained a position to teach at the Dominican nuns' school in <a href="/wiki/Speyer" title="Speyer">Speyer</a> from 1923 to 1931. While there, Stein translated <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>' <i>De Veritate</i> (<i>Of Truth</i>) into <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>, familiarized herself with Catholic philosophy in general and tried to bridge the phenomenology of her former teacher, Husserl, to <a href="/wiki/Thomism" title="Thomism">Thomism</a>. She visited Husserl and Heidegger at Freiburg in April 1929, the same month that Heidegger gave a speech to Husserl on his 70th birthday. In 1932 she became a lecturer at the Catholic Church-affiliated Institute for Scientific <a href="/wiki/Pedagogy" title="Pedagogy">Pedagogy</a> in <a href="/wiki/M%C3%BCnster" title="Münster">Münster</a>, but <a href="/wiki/Antisemitism" title="Antisemitism">antisemitic</a> legislation passed by the <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi government</a> forced her to resign the post in 1933. In a letter to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a>, she denounced the Nazi regime and asked the Pope to openly denounce the regime "to put a stop to this abuse of Christ's name."
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>As a child of the Jewish people who, by the grace of God, for the past eleven years has also been a child of the Catholic Church, I dare to speak to the Father of Christianity about that which oppresses millions of Germans. For weeks we have seen deeds perpetrated in Germany which mock any sense of justice and humanity, not to mention love of neighbor. For years the leaders of National Socialism have been preaching hatred of the Jews. … But the responsibility must fall, after all, on those who brought them to this point and it also falls on those who keep silent in the face of such happenings.
Everything that happened and continues to happen on a daily basis originates with a government that calls itself 'Christian'. For weeks not only Jews but also thousands of faithful Catholics in Germany, and, I believe, all over the world, have been waiting and hoping for the Church of Christ to raise its voice to put a stop to this abuse of Christ's name. Is not this idolization of race and governmental power which is being pounded into the public consciousness by the radio open heresy? Isn't the effort to destroy Jewish blood an abuse of the holiest humanity of our Savior, of the most blessed Virgin and the apostles? Is not all this diametrically opposed to the conduct of our Lord and Savior, who, even on the cross, still prayed for his persecutors? And isn't this a black mark on the record of this Holy Year which was intended to be a year of peace and reconciliation? We all, who are faithful children of the Church and who see the conditions in Germany with open eyes, fear the worst for the prestige of the Church, if the silence continues any longer.</p></blockquote>
<p>Her letter received no answer, and it is not known for certain whether the Pope ever saw it.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> However, in 1937 the Pope issued an encyclical written in German, <i><a href="/wiki/Mit_brennender_Sorge" title="Mit brennender Sorge">Mit brennender Sorge</a></i> (according to its German first words, lit. "With deep anxiety"<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>), in which he criticized Nazism, listed violations of the <a href="/wiki/Reichskonkordat" title="Reichskonkordat">Concordat</a> between Germany and the Church of 1933, and condemned antisemitism.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Discalced_Carmelite_nun_and_martyr">Discalced Carmelite nun and martyr</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Discalced Carmelite nun and martyr">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Stein entered the <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelite" class="mw-redirect" title="Discalced Carmelite">Discalced Carmelite</a> monastery St. Maria vom Frieden (Our Lady of Peace) in <a href="/wiki/Lindenthal,_Cologne" title="Lindenthal, Cologne">Cologne-Lindenthal</a> in October 1933 and took the <a href="/wiki/Religious_name" title="Religious name">religious name</a> <i>Teresia Benedicta a Cruce</i> (Teresa Benedicta of the Cross). In Cologne she wrote her <a href="/wiki/Metaphysical" class="mw-redirect" title="Metaphysical">metaphysical</a> book <i>Endliches und ewiges Sein</i> (<i>Finite and Eternal Being</i>), which attempted to combine the philosophies of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duns_Scotus" title="Duns Scotus">Duns Scotus</a> and Husserl.
</p><p>
To avoid the growing <a href="/wiki/Nazism" title="Nazism">Nazi</a> threat, the Order transferred Edith and her sister, Rosa, who was also a convert and an extern sister of the Carmel, to the Discalced Carmelite monastery in <a href="/wiki/Echt,_Limburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Echt, Limburg">Echt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>. There she wrote <i>Studie über Joannes a Cruce: Kreuzeswissenschaft</i> ("Studies on <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a>: The Science of the Cross"). In her testament of 9 June 1939<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> she wrote: <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I beg the Lord to take my life and my death … for all concerns of the sacred hearts of Jesus and Mary and the holy church, especially for the preservation of our holy order, in particular the Carmelite monasteries of Cologne and Echt, as atonement for the unbelief of the Jewish People, and that the Lord will be received by his own people and his kingdom shall come in glory, for the salvation of Germany and the peace of the world, at last for my loved ones, living or dead, and for all God gave to me: that none of them shall go astray.</p></blockquote>
<p>Stein's move to Echt prompted her to be more devout and even more observant of the Carmelite rule. After having her teaching position revoked by the implementation of the <a href="/wiki/Law_for_the_Restoration_of_the_Professional_Civil_Service" title="Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service">Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service</a>, Stein quickly eased back into the role of instructor at the convent in Echt, teaching both fellow sisters and students within the community Latin and philosophy.<sup id="cite_ref-Mosley,_J._2006_pp._43-52_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mosley,_J._2006_pp._43-52-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Even prior to the Nazi occupation of the Netherlands, Stein believed she would not survive the war, going so far as to write the Prioress to request her permission to "allow [Stein] to offer [her]self to the heart of Jesus as a sacrifice of atonement for true peace" and made a will. Her fellow sisters would later recount how Stein began "quietly training herself for life in a concentration camp, by enduring cold and hunger" after the Nazi invasion of the Netherlands in May 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-Mosley,_J._2006_pp._43-52_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mosley,_J._2006_pp._43-52-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ultimately, she would not be safe in the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_Nazi_Germany" title="Catholic Church and Nazi Germany">Dutch Bishops' Conference</a> had a public statement read in all churches across the nation on 20 July 1942 condemning Nazi <a href="/wiki/Racism" title="Racism">racism</a>. In a retaliatory response on 26 July 1942 the <a href="/wiki/Reichskommissar" title="Reichskommissar">Reichskommissar</a> of the Netherlands, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Seyss-Inquart" title="Arthur Seyss-Inquart">Arthur Seyss-Inquart</a>, ordered the arrest of all Jewish converts who had previously been spared. Along with two hundred and forty-three baptized Jews living in the Netherlands, Stein was arrested by the <a href="/wiki/SS" class="mw-redirect" title="SS">SS</a> on 2 August 1942. Stein and her sister Rosa were imprisoned at the concentration camps of <a href="/wiki/Amersfoort_concentration_camp" class="mw-redirect" title="Amersfoort concentration camp">Amersfoort</a> and <a href="/wiki/Westerbork_transit_camp" title="Westerbork transit camp">Westerbork</a> before being deported to Auschwitz. A Dutch official at Westerbork was so impressed by her sense of faith and calm<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> he offered her an escape plan. Stein vehemently refused his assistance, stating: "If somebody intervened at this point and took away [her] chance to share in the fate of [her] brothers and sisters, that would be utter annihilation."<sup id="cite_ref-Mosley,_J._2006_pp._43-52_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Mosley,_J._2006_pp._43-52-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 7 August 1942, early in the morning, 987 Jews were deported to the <a href="/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp" title="Auschwitz concentration camp">Auschwitz concentration camp</a>. It was probably on 9 August that Sister Teresa Benedicta of the Cross, her sister Rosa, and many more Jewish people were killed in a <a href="/wiki/Gas_chamber" title="Gas chamber">gas chamber</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-VNS_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-VNS-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Philosophy">Philosophy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Philosophy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Stein's development as a philosopher is frequently divided into three periods: an early, phenomenological (1916–25), a middle, comparative (1925–33) and a late, Christian (1935–42). In reality the same factors work themselves out throughout her work and propels it forward: 1. a profound understanding of and commitment to the phenomenological method as taught by Husserl and Reinach; 2. a deep sense of responsibility to the other for what we believe and 3. an acceptance of my own inability to form a complete, meaningful worldview without divine assistance. The three periods are best understood as stages of integration of these three factors, with Stein's baptism New Year's Day 1922, marking a decisive step on the way and her entering Carmel 14 October 1933 marking another. 
</p>
<h3><span id="The_early_phenomenological_period_.281916.E2.80.9325.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_early_phenomenological_period_(1916–25)">The early phenomenological period (1916–25)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: The early phenomenological period (1916–25)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Stein's dissertation on empathy was according to her own account an attempt to fill a gap in Husserl's work. In her autobiographical <i>Life in a Jewish Family</i>, she recalled that he took empathy to be the crucial act in which intersubjectivity was established, but nowhere detailed exactly what was meant by it. She therefore wanted to undertake this task and thereby clarify this crucial idea for the development of the phenomenological movement. While working as Husserl's assistant (1916–18) she edited Husserl's manuscripts of what was later to be published as <i>Ideas II</i> and <i>III</i>, and in the process came to understand the extraordinary importance this act has for our constitution of the intersubjective world, and in particular for the objects studied by psychology and the humanities. When she resigned from her position as Husserl's assistant, the phenomenological constitution of those objects: the psyche and the spirit, it was thus the first work she undertook. The result was the two treatises of <i>Philosophy of Psychology and the Humanities</i>, published in Husserl's <i>Jahrbuch</i> 1922: <i>Psychic Causality</i> and <i>Individual and Community</i>. From this period also dates <i>Introduction to Philosophy,</i> <i>An Investigation Concerning the State,</i> and very importantly <i>Freedom and Grace</i>. 
</p>
<h3><span id="The_middle_comparative_period_.281925.E2.80.9333.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_middle_comparative_period_(1925–33)">The middle comparative period (1925–33)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: The middle comparative period (1925–33)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Encouraged to study and compare Thomas Aquinas' philosophy with that of the phenomenological movement, Stein embarked on a translation project of Aquinas' <i>De Veritate</i>, which was to be published in two volumes in 1932. The work, which translates Aquinas' way of thinking into a modern German idiom and restyles it as a contemporary academic treatise, occasioned that Stein engaged with Aquinas' thought as a phenomenologist, i.e. as someone interested in the matters discussed by Aquinas, as distinct from providing an interpretation of Aquinas' thought or writing in prolongation of it as a thomist. The most important works from this period are 'Husserl and Aquinas: A Comparison', in which she discusses the differing methodologies of Husserl and Aquinas and accounts for their differences, <i>Potency and Act</i>, in which she attempts a phenomenological investigation of 'potency' and 'act' and the twin work of anthropology: The <i>Structure of the Human Person</i>. <i>Philosophical Anthropology</i> and <i>What is the Human Being?</i> <i>Theological Anthropology</i> (the second volume remains a highly developed draft rather than a completed work, since Stein's lectures were canceled in 1933). During this period she also lectures on women's education and vocation and on education in general to very large audiences and to great acclaim. In these lectures, published in ESGA 13 and ESGA 16, she works out for herself the important questions concerning social type and essence, which find a fuller development in <i>The Structure of the Human Person</i>.
</p>
<h3><span id="The_later_Christian_period_.281934.E2.80.9342.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_later_Christian_period_(1934–42)">The later Christian period (1934–42)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: The later Christian period (1934–42)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The first task Stein was assigned in the monastery was the writing of her incomplete autobiography, <i>Life in a Jewish Family</i>, a confession of her life as much as an apology in the literal sense for being of Jewish descent. Her next assignment was to prepare <i>Potency and Act</i> for publication, a task she accomplished by writing a new book: <i>Finite and Eternal Being – An Ascent to the Meaning of Being</i>. This work proposed a phenomenological doctrine of being <i>(Seinslehre)</i>, which knows itself to be Christian, i.e. as taking Christian Revelation to contribute towards the view of the world in which it looks for and finds the meaning of being in being's unfolding. Stein also worked on <a href="/wiki/Dionysius_the_Areopagite" title="Dionysius the Areopagite">Dionysius the Areopagite</a>, translating his works into German and writing (for him) a work supposed to be lost on symbolic theology. Stein's final work, the <i>Science of the Cross</i>, was a commentary on <a href="/wiki/John_of_the_Cross" title="John of the Cross">John of the Cross</a>, which developed the specifically Carmelite understanding of the depths of the soul, already of interest to Stein in her early work.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_veneration">Legacy and veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy and veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background-color:gold"><a href="/wiki/Metaphysics" title="Metaphysics">Metaphysics</a></div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:#ddf;text-align:center;">Practices</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content" style="padding-left:0.5em;padding-right:0.5em;">
<dl><dt>Monasticism<br /></dt></dl>

<p>Teresa Benedicta of the Cross was beatified as a martyr on 1 May 1987 in <a href="/wiki/Cologne" title="Cologne">Cologne</a>, Germany, by <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a> and then canonized by him 11 years later on 11 October 1998 in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>. The miracle that was the basis for her canonization is the cure of Benedicta McCarthy, a little girl who had swallowed a large amount of <a href="/wiki/Paracetamol" title="Paracetamol">paracetamol</a> (<a href="/wiki/Acetaminophen" class="mw-redirect" title="Acetaminophen">acetaminophen</a>), which causes <a href="/wiki/Hepatic_vein" class="mw-redirect" title="Hepatic vein">hepatic</a> <a href="/wiki/Necrosis" title="Necrosis">necrosis</a>. The young girl's father, <a href="/wiki/Emmanuel_Charles_McCarthy" title="Emmanuel Charles McCarthy">Emmanuel Charles McCarthy</a>, a priest of the <a href="/wiki/Melkite_Greek_Catholic_Church" title="Melkite Greek Catholic Church">Melkite Greek Catholic Church</a>, immediately called together relatives and prayed for Teresa's intercession.<sup id="cite_ref-Toronto_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Toronto-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Shortly thereafter the nurses in the intensive care unit saw her sit up, completely healthy. Ronald Kleinman, a pediatric specialist at <a href="/wiki/Massachusetts_General_Hospital" title="Massachusetts General Hospital">Massachusetts General Hospital</a> in <a href="/wiki/Boston" title="Boston">Boston</a> who treated the girl, testified about her recovery to church tribunals, stating: "I was willing to say that it was miraculous."<sup id="cite_ref-Toronto_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Toronto-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> McCarthy would later attend Sr. Teresa Benedicta's canonization.
</p><p>Teresa Benedicta of the Cross is one of the six <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saints</a> of <a href="/wiki/Europe" title="Europe">Europe</a>, together with <a href="/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia" title="Benedict of Nursia">Benedict of Nursia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cyril_and_Methodius" title="Cyril and Methodius">Cyril and Methodius</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bridget_of_Sweden" title="Bridget of Sweden">Bridget of Sweden</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena" title="Catherine of Siena">Catherine of Siena</a>.
</p><p>Today there are many schools named in tribute to her, for example in her hometown, <a href="/wiki/Lubliniec" title="Lubliniec">Lubliniec</a>, Poland <sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Darmstadt" title="Darmstadt">Darmstadt</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Hengelo" title="Hengelo">Hengelo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Mississauga" title="Mississauga">Mississauga</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a>, Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Also named for her are a women's dormitory at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_T%C3%BCbingen" title="University of Tübingen">University of Tübingen</a><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and a classroom building at <a href="/wiki/The_College_of_the_Holy_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="The College of the Holy Cross">The College of the Holy Cross</a> in <a href="/wiki/Worcester,_Massachusetts" title="Worcester, Massachusetts">Worcester, Massachusetts</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Lubliniec" title="Lubliniec">Lubliniec</a> in Poland hosts <a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein_Museum&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Edith Stein Museum (page does not exist)">Edith Stein Museum</a> (Muzeum Pro Memoria Edith Stein) localised on the first floor of the Courant family house (Edith Stein's grandparents' family home).  <a href="/wiki/Wroc%C5%82aw" title="Wrocław">Wroclaw</a> hosts a museum called Edith Stein House localised in the house Edith's mother bought for the family in 1919 on the street then called Michaelisstrasse 38 (today <a href="/wiki/Nowowiejski" title="Nowowiejski">Nowowiejska</a> 38).
</p><p>In Vienna, the Edith-Stein-Haus at Ebendorferstraße 8 is the main location of the Catholic University Chaplaincy and the university pastoral care of the Archdiocese of Vienna. In the spirit of Karl Strobl's model of the "Catholic Student House", the house is also home to a chapel consecrated to Edith Stein as well as a dormitory for about 90 students.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The philosopher <a href="/wiki/Alasdair_MacIntyre" title="Alasdair MacIntyre">Alasdair MacIntyre</a> published a book in 2006 titled <i>Edith Stein: A Philosophical Prologue, 1913–1922</i>, in which he contrasted her living of her own personal philosophy with <a href="/wiki/Martin_Heidegger" title="Martin Heidegger">Martin Heidegger</a>, whose actions during the Nazi era, according to MacIntyre, suggested a "bifurcation of personality."<sup id="cite_ref-macstein_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-macstein-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Playwright Arthur Giron wrote <i>Edith Stein</i>, a play that was inspired by Stein's life. It was produced at the <a href="/wiki/Pittsburgh_Public_Theater" title="Pittsburgh Public Theater">Pittsburgh Public Theater</a> in 1988.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1988, Edith Stein was pictured on a German postage stamp with <a href="/wiki/Rupert_Mayer" title="Rupert Mayer">Rupert Mayer</a> SJ in honor of their beatification.
</p><p>In 1991 the Italian musician <a href="/wiki/Juri_Camisasca" title="Juri Camisasca">Juri Camisasca</a> released a song inspired by the life of Edith Stein, <i>Il carmelo di Echt</i> (The carmelite convent of Echt) on the album of the same name. The song was later recorded by <a href="/wiki/Giuni_Russo" title="Giuni Russo">Giuni Russo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Franco_Battiato" title="Franco Battiato">Franco Battiato</a>.
</p><p>In 1995, Hungarian film director <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1rta_M%C3%A9sz%C3%A1ros" title="Márta Mészáros">Márta Mészáros</a> made a movie about the life and death of Edith Stein with the title <i><a href="/wiki/The_Seventh_Chamber" class="mw-redirect" title="The Seventh Chamber">A hetedik szoba</a></i> (The Seventh Room/Chamber), starring <a href="/wiki/Maia_Morgenstern" title="Maia Morgenstern">Maia Morgenstern</a>.
</p><p>In 1999, a memorial statue by German sculptor Bert Gerresheim was dedicated in Cologne, Germany. The statue comprises three different views of Stein reflecting her Jewish and Christian faith, and a pile of empty shoes representing the victims of the holocaust.
</p><p>In 2007, Stein's life and work was dramatised in the novel <i>Winter Under Water</i> (Picador, London) by author James Hopkin.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2008, the first <i><a href="/wiki/Stolperstein" title="Stolperstein">Stolperstein</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Polish_language" title="Polish language">Polish</a>: <i lang="pl">kamienie pamięci</i>) that was ever laid in Poland was placed near Edith Stein's childhood home at 38 ul. Nowowiejska (formerly the Michaelisstraße) in Wrocław. Other <i>Stolpersteine</i> for her are in Cologne (several) and Freiburg.
</p><p>In 2009, her <a href="/wiki/Bust_(sculpture)" title="Bust (sculpture)">bust</a> was installed at the <a href="/wiki/Walhalla_Memorial" class="mw-redirect" title="Walhalla Memorial">Walhalla Memorial</a> near <a href="/wiki/Regensburg" title="Regensburg">Regensburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a>. In June 2009 the International Association for the Study of the Philosophy of Edith Stein (IASPES) was founded, and held its first international conference at Maynooth University, Ireland, in order to advance the philosophical writings of Stein.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 6 June 2014, the 70th anniversary of <a href="/wiki/D-Day" class="mw-redirect" title="D-Day">D-Day</a>, a bell dedicated to her was named by <a href="/wiki/Prince_Charles" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince Charles">Prince Charles</a> at <a href="/wiki/Bayeux" title="Bayeux">Bayeux</a> Cathedral.
</p><p>Also in 2014, the book <i>Edith Stein and <a href="/wiki/Regina_Jonas" title="Regina Jonas">Regina Jonas</a>: Religious Visionaries in the Time of the Death Camps</i>, by Emily Leah Silverman, was published.
</p><p>In 2018, American film director <a href="/wiki/Joshua_Sinclair" title="Joshua Sinclair">Joshua Sinclair</a> made a movie about the life and death of Edith Stein with the title <i>A Rose in Winter</i>, starring Zana Marjanovic.
</p><p>In 2022, Stein was officially added to the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church liturgical calendar</a> with a feast day on 9 August.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Controversy_as_to_the_cause_of_her_murder">Controversy as to the cause of her murder</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Controversy as to the cause of her murder">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatification</a> of Teresa Benedicta as a <a href="/wiki/Martyr#Christianity" title="Martyr">martyr</a> generated criticism. Critics argued that she was murdered because she was Jewish by birth, rather than for her Christian faith,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> and that, in the words of Daniel Polish, the beatification seemed to "carry the tacit message encouraging conversionary activities" because "official discussion of the beatification seemed to make a point of conjoining Stein's Catholic faith with her death with 'fellow Jews' in Auschwitz."<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The position of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> is that Teresa Benedicta also died because of the Dutch episcopacy's public condemnation of Nazi racism in 1942; in other words, that she died because of the moral teaching of the church and is thus a true martyr.<sup id="cite_ref-VNS_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-VNS-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Bibliography">Bibliography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Bibliography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>For a detailed chronology of Stein's writings see <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.edithsteincircle.com/biography/chronology-of-writings.html">IASPES' website</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Primary_literature">Primary literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Primary literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="In_German">In German</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: In German">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<ul><li>1917, <i>Zum Problem der Einfühlung</i> Halle: <i>Buchdruckerei des Waisenhauses</i>. (<a href="/wiki/Thesis" title="Thesis">Doctoral Thesis</a>).</li>
<li>1916-1220, <i>Einführung in die Philosophie</i>, lectures taught at a proseminar in Freiburg in 1916–1918 and, later, privately in Breslau in <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://edytastein.org.pl/en/">Edith Stein House</a> in 1920<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>1921, <i>Freiheit und Gnade</i>. This work has for years been wrongly identified and quoted as <i>Die ontische Struktur der Person und ihre erkenntnistheoretische Problematik</i>, a title that appeared in print after WWII due to an incorrect connection between title page and work.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>1922, <i>Beiträge zur philosophischen Begründung der Psychologie und der Geisteswissenschaften</i>, in <i>Jahrbuch für Philosophie und phänomenologische Forschung</i> 5, Halle: Max Niemeyer, 1-284.</li>
<li>1924, <i>Was ist Phänomenologie?</i></li>
<li>1924, <i>Was ist Philosophie? Ein Gespräch zwischen Edmund Husserl und Thomas von Aquino</i></li>
<li>1925, <i>Eine Untersuchung über den Staat</i>, in <i>Jahrbuch für Philosophie und phänomenologische Forschung</i> 7, Halle: Max Niemeyer, 1–123.</li>
<li>1929, <i>Husserls Phänomenologie und die Philosophie des heiligen Thomas von Aquino. Versuch einer Gegenüberstellung</i>, in <i>Festschrift Edmund Husserl zum 70. Geburtstag gewidmet</i>, (<i>Jahrbuch für Philosophie und phänomenologische Forschung</i> 10), Ergänzungsband, Halle: Max Niemeyer, 315–338.</li>
<li>1930/1931, <i>Die weltanschauliche Bedeutung der Phänomenologie</i></li>
<li>1931, <i>Potenz und Akt. Studien zu einer Philosophie des Seins</i></li>
<li>1932, <i>Der Aufbau der menschlichen Person. Vorlesung zur philosophischen Anthropologie</i></li>
<li>1933, <i>Was ist der Mensch? Theologische Anthropologie.</i> <i>Das Menschenbild unseres Glaube</i>ns</li>
<li>1928–1933, <i>Die Frau. Fragestellungen und Reflexionen</i></li>
<li>1935/1936, <i>Endliches und ewiges Sein. Versuch eines Aufstiegs zum Sinn des Seins,</i> written with two supplements:
<ul><li><i>Die Seelenburg zu Endliches und ewiges Sein</i></li>
<li><i>Martin Heidegger’s Existenzphilosophie</i></li></ul></li>
<li>1940/1941, <i>Wege der Gotteserkenntnis. Studie zu Dionysius Areopagita</i></li>
<li>1941/1942, <i>Kreuzeswissenschaft. Studie über Johannes vom Kreuz</i></li>
<li>1962, <i>Welt und Person</i> (posthumous publication)</li></ul>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Contemporary_critical_edition">Contemporary critical edition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Contemporary critical edition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>(<i>Edith Stein Gesamtausgabe</i>, Herder 2000–2020) with English and Polish translations available<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>ESGA 1: Stein E., <i>Aus dem Leben einer jüdischen Familie</i> <i>und weitere autobiographische Beiträge</i>, Herder, Freiburg 2002.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 1: <i>Life in a Jewish Family: Her Unfinished Autobiographical Account</i>, trans. Josephine Koeppel in: <i>The Collected Works of Edith Stein. Volume 1</i>, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 1986;</li>
<li>Polish translation: ESGA PL 1: <i>Dzieje pewnej rodziny żydowskiej</i>, trans. Immakulata J. Adamska, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2005.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 2: Stein E., <i>Selbstbildnis in Briefen I. Erster Teil 1916–1933</i>, Herder, Freiburg 2000.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 5: <i>Self-portrait in Letters 1916–1942</i>, trans. Josephine Koeppel in: <i>The Collected Works of Edith Stein. Volume 1</i>, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 1993.</li>
<li>Polish translation: <i>Autoportret z listów I</i>, trans. Immakulata J. Adamska, Anna Talarek, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2003.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 3: Stein E., <i>Selbstbildnis in Briefen II. Zweiter Teil 1933–1942</i>, Herder, Freiburg 2000.
<ol><li>Polish translation: ESGA PL 3: <i>Autoportret z listów II,</i> trans. Immakulata J. Adamska, Anna Talarek, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2003.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 4: Stein E., <i>Selbstbildnis in Briefen III. Briefe an Roman Ingarden</i>, Herder, Freiburg 2005.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 12: <i>Self-Portrait in Letters</i>, trans. Hugh Candler Hunt, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2001.</li>
<li>Polish translation ESGA PL 4: <i>Autoportret z listów. Cz. 3. Listy do Romana Ingardena</i> trans. Małgorzata Klentak-Zabłocka, Andrzej Wajs, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2003; an older Polish translation: PL 4: <i>Spór o prawdę istnienia. Listy Edith Stein do Romana Ingardena</i>, trans. Małgorzata Klentak-Zabłocka, Andrzej Wajs, Wydawnictwo M, Warsaw 1994.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 5: Stein E., <i>Zum Problem der Einfühlung</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2008.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 3: <i>On the problem of empathy</i>, trans. Waltraut Stein, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 1989.</li>
<li>Polish translation: <i>O zagadnieniu wczucia</i>, trans. Danuta Gierulanka, Jerzy F. Gierula, Znak, Cracow 1988.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 6: Stein E., <i>Beiträge zur philosophischen Begründung der Psychologie und der Geisteswissenschaften</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2010.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 7: <i>Philosophy of Psychology and the Humanities</i>, trans. Mary Catharine Baseheart, Marianne Sawicki, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2000;</li>
<li>Polish translation: ESGA PL 6: <i>Filozofia psychologii i humanistyki</i>, trans. Piotr Janik SJ, Marcin Baran SJ, Jolanta Gaca, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2016.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 7: Stein E., <i>Eine Untersuchung über den Staat</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2006.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 10: <i>An Investigation Concerning the State</i>, trans. Marianne Sawicki, ICS Publication, Washington D.C. 2006.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 8: Stein E., <i>Einführung in die Philosophie</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2004.
<ol><li>No translation is available.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 9: Stein E., <i>‘Freiheit und Gnade’ und weitere Beiträge zu Phänomenologie und Ontologie</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2014.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 8: <i>Husserl and Aquinas. A Comparison</i>, in: CWES 8: <i>Knowledge and Faith</i>, trans. Walter Redmond, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2000, pp. 1–64. Older translation is available in: M. C. Baseheart, <i>Person in the World: Introduction to the Philosophy of Edith Stein</i>, Kluwer, Dordrecht 1997, pp. 129–144, transl. by M. C. Baseheart.</li>
<li>Polish translation of <i>Was Ist Philosophie? Ein Gespräch zwischen Edmund Husserl und Thomas von Aquino</i> is <i>Co to jest filozofia? Rozmowa między Edmundem Husserlem a Tomaszem z Akwinu</i>, in: PL 9: <i>Światło rozumu i wiary. Duchowa droga Edyty Stein św. Teresy Benedykty od Krzyża</i>, Totaldruk, Poznań 2002, pp. 29–77.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 10: Stein E., <i>Potenz und Akt. Studien zu einer Philosophie des Seins</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2005.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 11: <i>Potency and Act, Studies Toward a Philosophy of Being</i>, trans. Walter Redmond, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2009.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 11/12: Stein E., <i>Endliches und ewiges Sein. Versuch eines Aufstiegs zum Sinn des Seins. Anhang: Martin Heideggers Existenzphilosophie. Die Seelenburg</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2006.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 9: <i>Finite and eternal being</i>, trans. K. F. Reinhardt, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2002.</li>
<li>Polish translation: PL 11/12a: <i>Byt skończony a byt wieczny</i>, trans. Immakulata J. Adamska OCD, W drodze, Poznań 1995. The appendixes (<i>Die Seelenburg</i> and <i>Martin Heideggers Existenzphilosophie</i>) are translated in: PL 11/12b: <i>Twierdza duchowa,</i> trans. Immakulata J. Adamska, Zysk i S-ka, Poznań 2006, 93–122 and 135–203.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 13: Stein E., <i>Die Frau. Fragestellungen und Reflexionen</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2000, Herder.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 2: <i>The Collected Works of Edith Stein. Volume II. Essays on Woman</i>, trans. F. M. Oben, Washington D.C. 1996, ICS Publications.</li>
<li>Polish translation: ESGA PL 13: <i>Kobieta. Pytania i refleksje</i>, trans. Wiesław Szymona, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2015.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 14: Stein E., <i>Der Aufbau der menschlichen Person. Vorlesung zum philosophischen Anthropologie</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 1994, Herder, Freiburg 2004.
<ol><li>Polish translation: ESGA PL 14: <i>Budowa osoby ludzkiej. Wykład z antropologii filozoficznej</i>, trans. Grzegorz Sowinski, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2015.</li>
<li>Italian translation: <i>La struttura della persona umana</i>, trans. L. Gelber-M. Linssen, M. d’Ambra, Città Nuova, Rome 2000.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 15: Stein E., <i>Was is der Mensch? Theologische Anthropologie</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2005, Herder.
<ol><li>Polish translation: ESGA PL 15: <i>Czym jest człowiek? Antropologia teologiczna</i>, trans. Grzegorz Sowinski, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2012.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 16: Stein E., <i>Bildung und Entfaltung der Individualität. Beiträge zum christlichen</i> <i>Erziehungsauftrag</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2001.</li>
<li>ESGA 17: Stein E., <i>Wege der Gotteserkenntnis. Studie zu Dionysius Areopagita und Übersetzung seiner Werke</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2013.
<ol><li>Polish translation: <i>Drogi poznania Boga: studium o Dionizym Areopagicie i przekład jego dzieł,</i> trans. Grzegorz Sowinski, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2006.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 18: <i>Kreuzeswissenschaft.</i> <i>Studie über Johannes vom Kreuz</i>, Herder, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2003.
<ol><li>English translation: CWES 6: <i>The Science of the Cross</i>, trans. Josephine Koeppel, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2002;</li>
<li>Polish translation: <i>Wiedza Krzyża. Studium o św. Janie od Krzyża,</i> trans. Immakulata J. Adamska, Grzegorz Sowinski, Wydawnictwo Karmelitów Bosych, Cracow 2013.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 19: <i>Geistliche Texte I</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2009, Herder.
<ol><li>English translation: <i>The Hidden Life: Essays, Meditations, Spiritual Texts</i>, trans. Waltraut Stein in: <i>The Collected Works of Edith Stein. Volume IV</i>, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2014.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 20: <i>Geistliche Texte II</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2007, Herder.
<ol><li>English translation: ESGA EN 4: <i>The Hidden Life: Essays, Meditations, Spiritual Texts</i>, trans. Waltraut Stein in: <i>The Collected Works of Edith Stein. Volume IV</i>, ICS Publications, Washington D.C. 2014.</li></ol></li>
<li>ESGA 21: <i>Übersetzung von John Henry Newman, Die Idee der Universität</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2014, Herder.</li>
<li>ESGA 22: <i>Übersetzung von John Henry Newman, Briefe und Texte zur ersten Lebenshälfte (1801–1846)</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2004, Herder.</li>
<li>ESGA 23: <i>Übersetzung: Des Hl. Thomas von Aquino Untersuchungen über die Wahrheit – Quaestiones disputatae de veritate 1</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2014, Herder.</li>
<li>ESGA 24: <i>Übersetzung: Des Hl. Thomas von Aquino Untersuchungen über die Wahrheit – Quaestiones disputatae de veritate 2</i>, Freiburg-Basel-Wien 2002, Herder.</li>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Edith_Stein&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-14"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-14">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Parts II–IV of the dissertation were published under the title <i>Zum Problem der Einfühlung</i> (<i>On the Problem of Empathy</i>) in 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In his 2007 M.A. thesis, "The Philosophical Contributions of Edith Stein",<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> John C. Wilhelmsson argues that Stein influenced the work of Husserl significantly during this period.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
